--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time (~400-600 steps): robots should be STATIONARY (Stop) to minimize score
- After switch time: robots should be MOVING to minimize score
- The green wall lights up at switch time - robots should detect it with color sensor (C=7, color=2 for green) and switch from Stop to moving

**Analysis of the champion (score 11656.20):**
- State 0: Repulsion (moving away from neighbors) - this is the "moving" phase
- State 1: Stop - this is the "stationary" phase
- Transitions from Stop (s1) to Repulsion (s0): when neighbors > 1 (C=3, p=1) OR gray floor with prob 0.6645
- Transitions from Repulsion (s0) to Stop (s1): various neighbor conditions + gray floor

Wait, let me re-read. The champion has State 0=Repulsion and State 1=Stop. The transitions from State 1 (Stop) back to State 0 (Repulsion) use neighbor count C=3 and gray floor C=1. This seems counterintuitive for the mission.

Actually the key insight: we want robots to be stationary BEFORE the switch, then moving AFTER. The green wall is the signal. The champion doesn't use C=7 (color perception) at all!

The champion seems to be doing well by having robots mostly stop (State 1) and occasionally moving (State 0). The repulsion keeps them spread out when moving.

**Strategy for round 2:**
1. Keep the champion exactly
2. Exploit: small variations of the champion (adjust probabilities/thresholds)
3. Explore: machines that explicitly detect green (C=7, l=2) to trigger movement after switch
4. Try: Stop initially, then GoToColor/Exploration when green is detected

Key: C=7 with l=2 (green) would detect the lit wall and trigger movement.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 5 --rep0 3.2298 --cle0 5 --n0 4 --n0x0 3 --c0x0 4 --p0x0 7 --w0x0 16.0303 --n0x1 2 --c0x1 3 --p0x1 2 --w0x1 1.0711 --n0x2 2 --c0x2 3 --p0x2 7 --w0x2 5.0919 --n0x3 0 --c0x3 1 --p0x3 0.1204 --s1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 3 --p1x0 1 --w1x0 6.0293 --n1x1 2 --c1x1 1 --p1x1 0.6645

Now let me design good alternatives:

**Key design: Stop first, detect green (C=7, l=2) → move**

FSM ideas:
- 2 states: Stop → Exploration, triggered by seeing green (C=7, l=2)
- 2 states: Stop → Repulsion, triggered by green
- 3 states: Stop → GoToColor(green) → Exploration

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The top performers all share a clear pattern:
- **State 0: Stop** (B=1), with transitions triggered by:
  - Seeing green (C=7, l=2, p≈0.9-0.95) → go to movement state
  - Many neighbors (C=3, p=8, w=15) → go to movement state  
  - Very low random prob (C=5, p≈0.001-0.002) → go to movement state (fallback)
- **State 1: Movement** (Exploration B=0, or Repulsion B=5), with transitions back to Stop when:
  - Neighbors below threshold (C=4, p=1, w=10-12) → back to stop

The champion (#1, score 10534.65):
- Stop → Exploration (rwm=40) triggered by green/many-neighbors/random-tiny
- Exploration → Stop when neighbors < 1 (C=4, p=1, w=12) OR tiny random

This makes perfect sense: robots stop before switch, then the green wall lights up, they detect it and start moving.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary probabilities, thresholds, rwm in the champion topology
3. Add: try neighbor-above condition to also trigger movement (robots clustering might signal switch?)
4. Try: higher green detection probability, different movement behaviors
5. The 4-state machine (#3) also worked well - keep similar structure

Key parameters to tune:
- Green detection: p=0.95 seems good, also try 0.99
- Random fallback: p=0.001-0.002 is critical (too high = moves before switch)
- Neighbor threshold C=3 with p=8, w=15 seems good (many neighbors → move)
- Movement speed/type: Exploration rwm=30-50 seems better than Repulsion

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

The champion (10178.10) added a 4th transition from Stop: C=3 (neighbors above 5, w=12) in addition to green detection, many-neighbors (p=8,w=15), and tiny random. This extra neighbor trigger helps.

The top machines all converge on the same topology:
- State 0: Stop (B=1)
- State 1: Exploration (B=0, rwm≈30-50)
- Stop→Explore: green detected (C=7,l=2,p=0.95) + many neighbors (C=3) + tiny random (C=5,p=0.001)
- Explore→Stop: few neighbors (C=4,p=1,w=12) + tiny random (C=5,p=0.005)

Scores are tightly clustered (10178-10438 for top 11), suggesting we're near a local optimum. Need to fine-tune AND explore new topologies.

**Parameters to tune in champion topology:**
- The extra C=3 transition (p=5, w=12) — try p=4,6,7 and different w
- Neighbor threshold for C=4 back to Stop: try p=2,3
- rwm: 40 seems good, try 35, 45
- Green probability: 0.95 seems optimal
- Random prob in Stop: 0.001 seems right

**Exploration ideas:**
- 4-state: Stop → detect green → move fast → slow down
- Add green detection also in movement state (to reinforce staying moving)
- Try GoToColor green as movement behavior (actively seek the green wall)
- Different w values for neighbor conditions

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 4:**

The champion (9837.02) uses p0x3=4 (neighbors above 4, w=12) as the extra trigger. The previous champion used p=5. So lower neighbor threshold (4) is better than 5 for that 4th transition.

Interesting findings:
- p=4 (best) > p=5 > p=6 for the 4th neighbor trigger
- Adding green detection back in the exploration state (transition 3 in #2) helps a bit
- p=2 for C=4 back-to-stop threshold also seems useful (#3)
- w=12 for 4th trigger seems irrelevant (10, 12, 15 all give same score)

The C=4 (neighbors below threshold) back to Stop: p=1 (fewer than 1 neighbor) seems key. When robots are isolated they stop. This makes sense — before switch, robots scatter and stop; after switch when green detected they explore.

**Parameters to explore:**
- 4th trigger: try p=3 (even lower threshold)
- 4th trigger: try p=2 (very sensitive to clustering)
- w for 4th trigger: doesn't matter much, keep 12
- C=3 first trigger (p=8, w=15): try p=7, p=9
- Try adding 4th transition to exploration state too (green detection → keep moving)
- Try: neighbor threshold p=2 for C=4 back-to-stop
- Try varying w for the C=4 back-to-stop condition

The biggest gap is between #12 (10416) and #13 (11529) — 3-state machines with GoToColor seem bad. Stick to 2-state.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 5:**

The champion (9706.10) uses p0x3=3 (neighbors above 3, w=12). The runner-up uses p=2 (9787.93). So the trend is: lower neighbor thresholds for the 4th trigger are better (4→3→2 trend).

Critical observations:
- p=3 best, p=2 second best — try p=1 (above 1 neighbor)
- Multiple machines with identical scores (10180.17) for variants of p=4 with different w/rwm — these parameters don't matter much once you're past the optimum
- The w parameter for the 4th trigger (w0x3) seems irrelevant (10, 12, 15 all same)
- The w parameter for the C=4 back-to-stop (w1x0) is also irrelevant (10, 12, 15 all same)
- rwm matters a bit: 40 > 35 > 45
- Green prob: 0.95 seems slightly better than 0.97

**What to try:**
- p0x3=1 (trigger movement when ANY neighbor present)
- Combine p=3 with p=2 triggers (both transitions from stop)
- Try different w values for the main C=3 trigger (w0x1=15, try 10, 20)
- Try p0x1=8 vs other values with p0x3=3
- Add green detection in exploration state (back to stop when NOT seeing green — actually can't do that)
- Try combining champion topology with 3rd transition in exploration state

The scores 10-14 are all 10180.17 — exactly the same! This suggests these are all the same effective machine (the C=4 back-to-stop w parameter doesn't matter, as long as robots with 0 neighbors stop).

**Key insight:** The w parameter for C=4 (neighbors below 1) — with threshold=1 and any w, it fires when there are 0 neighbors. The w just affects the sigmoid steepness but since threshold=1 and neighbors are integers, it's essentially binary.

Focus on: p0x3 values (1, 2, 3) and combinations.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 6:**

The champion (9706.10) still holds from round 5. This round's best was 9818.74 (p1x1=0.008). The champion used p1x1=0.005.

Critical findings:
- p0x3=1 (above 1 neighbor) was catastrophically bad (11917.48) — this causes robots to move too early when they cluster at start
- p0x3=3 is best, p0x3=2 is second, p0x3=1 is terrible
- p1x1=0.008 (random back to stop from exploration) slightly worse than champion's 0.005
- p1x1=0.003 also slightly worse
- p0x2=0.002 (random fallback from stop) slightly worse than 0.001
- w0x3=8 and w0x3=20 perform similarly to w0x3=12
- p0x1=7 and p0x1=6 perform close to p0x1=8 — all within noise range
- The w parameter for C=3 trigger (w0x1) is truly irrelevant (10, 15, 20 all same)

**Key insight:** We're very close to the optimum for this topology. The scores 8-12 in this round (9895-9899) are essentially identical to each other. The champion at 9706 seems to have gotten lucky on a particular random seed set.

**Strategy for Round 7:**
- Keep champion exactly
- Small variations around champion: p0x3=3 is locked in, try small tweaks
- Try combining p0x3=3 AND p0x3=2 in separate transitions (but limited to 4 total)
- Try different rwm values (36, 38, 42)
- Try p0x0=0.93, 0.97 for green detection
- Try p1x0=1 with different w1x0 (already shown irrelevant)
- Explore: try adding a 3rd transition to exploration (green color seen → stop? No, that's wrong direction)
- Try: 4-state machine with intermediate "slow down" state
- Try: p0x1=5 (fewer neighbors needed to trigger)

Actually, the scores are very tight (9706-9899 range). Let me try to push further with novel approaches while keeping exploitation focus.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 7:**

The new champion (9512.65) is the 3-state machine! It has:
- State 0: Stop → transitions to State 1 (slow exploration, rwm=40) triggered by green/many-neighbors/random
- State 1: Exploration (rwm=40) → back to Stop (State 0) when few neighbors OR random; OR to State 2 when random(0.005)
- State 2: Exploration (rwm=60, faster turning) → back to State 1 when few neighbors; back to State 0 when sees green

Wait, let me re-read:
- s0=Stop, transitions all go to s1 (index 0 in "others" = state 1)
- s1=Exploration(rwm=40), n1x0→state 0 (C=4,p=1,w=12), n1x1→state 1 itself? No wait — destination 1 from state 1 with 3 states means the second "other" = state 2
  - n1x0: D=0 → state 0 (Stop), C=4 (neighbors<1)
  - n1x1: D=1 → state 2, C=5 (random 0.005)
- s2=Exploration(rwm=60), n2x0→state 1, n2x1→state 0 (when sees green)

So: Stop → Slow Explore → Fast Explore (randomly) → back to Slow Explore (when isolated) OR back to Stop (when green seen again, with p=0.95)

This is interesting! The 3-state structure adds a "fast exploration" phase. The green detection in state 2 creates a feedback loop.

**Strategy for Round 8:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary rwm values (40→35,45; 60→50,70,80), vary p2x1 (0.95→0.9,0.99)
3. Try: different movement behaviors in state 1 or 2 (repulsion?)
4. Try: swap state 1 and 2 behaviors (fast first, then slow)
5. Try: 4-state version adding another exploration level
6. Try: vary neighbor thresholds in state 2 return condition

Key parameters to tune in champion:
- rwm1=40: try 35, 38, 42, 45
- rwm2=60: try 50, 70, 80
- p2x1=0.95: try 0.9, 0.99 (green detection in state 2)
- p1x1=0.005 (random to state 2): try 0.003, 0.008, 0.01
- w2x0=12: try different values

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 8:**

The new champion (9324.95) added a 3rd transition to state 1 (exploration): when green is seen (C=7, l=2, p=0.95) → go to state 2 (fast exploration). This creates a reinforcing loop: once green appears, robots switch to fast exploration and stay there (state 2 also detects green to go back to state 1... wait, state 2 goes back to state 1 when isolated, and back to state 0 when green seen).

Actually re-reading: state 2 n2x1 goes to state 0 (Stop) when green seen with p=0.95. That seems wrong... but it scored best! Maybe the green detection in state 2 going back to Stop helps some robots re-anchor quickly?

Wait — n2x1: D=0 from state 2 with 3 states. Others from state 2 are [0,1] → D=0=state 0 (Stop), D=1=state 1 (slow explore). So state 2 goes back to Stop when green detected. This is counterintuitive but works.

Actually thinking more carefully: The green wall appears at switch time. Before switch, robots stop. After switch they need to move. If state 2 (fast exploration) detects green and goes BACK to stop... that seems bad. But maybe the transition is rarely triggered because probability fires AND then they keep moving most of the time.

**Top performers all have:**
- State 0: Stop with 4 triggers → State 1
- State 1: Slow explore (rwm=35-40) → back to Stop OR → State 2 (green detection p=0.95 added in champion)
- State 2: Fast explore (rwm=60-80) → back to State 1 (isolated) OR → State 0 (green seen)

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 9:**

The new champion (9160.86) adds a 3rd transition to state 2: C=3 (neighbors above 3, w=12) → go to state 1. This means state 2 (fast explore) goes back to state 1 when robots cluster (>3 neighbors) OR when isolated (C=4, p=1) OR when green seen (back to stop).

The top machines all score 9160-9307, tightly clustered. Key differences:
- Champion: state 2 has 3 transitions (isolated→s1, green→s0, cluster→s1)
- #2 (9171): rwm1=38, rwm2=70, state 2 has only 2 transitions
- #3 (9190): rwm2=80, 2 transitions in s2
- #4 (9222): state 1 has 4 transitions (added neighbor trigger)

**What the champion adds:** The extra C=3 (p=3, w=12) in state 2 creates a loop: fast-explore → slow-explore when clustering. This seems beneficial.

**Parameters to tune for final round:**
- Combine champion's 3-transition s2 with rwm variations (38, 42 for s1; 70, 80 for s2)
- Try adding the neighbor trigger to state 1 as 4th transition (like #4) PLUS the 3-transition s2
- Try different w2x2 values (8, 15, 20) for the new s2 neighbor trigger
- Try p2x2 threshold of 2 or 4 in s2
- Try p1x1=0.003 with champion topology
- Try combining all improvements: rwm1=38, rwm2=70, 3-transition s2

This is the last round — maximize exploitation of the champion topology with targeted parameter variations.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
